            In Response to Monetarist Critics1

David Laidler's long and careful review of this book greatly clarifies
some major issues. There are a number of points to which it may be
useful for me to respond. To do justice to them this Preface has to be
fairly long.
   In the book we felt it necessary to discuss, along with monetary
matters, many related aspects of macroeconomics. Everything being
interrelated, it is always difficult to know where to stop; and we felt we
could stop without saying much about inflation. That probably was a
mistake. For, as Laidler says, 'most monetary economists, particularly
North American monetary economists, would put the explanation of
the price level at the top of, or at least high on, the list of problems
with which their theory should deal'. For us, money does not determine
prices (that is the conclusion we reach, at the end of the line of argument
developed in the book); and that led us to leave inflation out. But to
many such a conclusion must seem to leave something important
hanging, namely: what, then, does determine the price level? Laidler
ends his review with an appeal to us or 'some of the many British
Keynesians who share [our] views' to define a more precise view on
inflation.
   This Preface may be seen as a response. It does not aim to give a
balanced summary of the argument of the book (that anyhow would
be a duplication). While some may prefer to read it after reading the
book itself, to others it may be a useful guide to the controversial
points.
   The considerations dictating the presentation are these.
   • Some readers have found the book plain going and what it says
      immediately recognizable as true. But others have missed part of
      the argument through not quite seeing the kind of monetary policy
      that the book is about, and Section 1 of this Preface therefore aims
      to provide a clear and unmistakable description.
   1

Journal, March. I have been greatly helped by comments on an earlier draft by Bryan Hopkin,
Andrew Britton, and David Laidler himself, though naturally I alone am responsible for the
present text. Brief references only will be given here to works already cited in the bibliography.
References to the present book are denoted by page number. References to my previous book,
The Management of the British Economy, are denoted as MBE. Quotations from Laidler are
from his review article.
vi                                  Preface
     • Section 2 restates our position on monetary-base control. This is
       a crucial issue on which Laidler rightly focuses. Our view of
       monetary-base control—which differs from that of many (perhaps
       most) academic economists but is in accord with what seems to be
       the view of all central banks—is that base control would be
       impractical and ineffective. How far our reasons depend on
       assumptions about the nature of inflation needs clarifying.
     • Section 3 discusses the monetarist claim that monetary growth
       controls inflation. That proposition assumes that monetary growth
       itself can be controlled, which is something that we consider
       possible only in a limited and temporary way.
     • Section 4 perhaps goes some way to meet David Laidler's request
       for a more precise view about inflation—though it may do little
       more than identify what evidence is relevant for what purpose.
     • Section 5 has some concluding remarks. The book may appear to
       devote much space to a rather narrowly defined sort of monetary
       policy; but that kind of policy, even though a failure in practice,
       is still much alive in men's minds. But the book is not concerned
       only to criticize one line of policy. More positively, it is concerned
       also with how central banks get their power—questions which are
       under-explored and have wide implications.
1. TWO TYPES OF MONETARY POLICY

Adoption of a policy of seeking to control the rate of growth of the
monetary aggregates, and announcing target rates of growth, came after
some three decades during which the government sought to use fiscal
and monetary policy to control aggregate demand. Demand management
having become discredited, a type of monetary policy framed in terms
of monetary targets was seen as a superior alternative. Its characteristics
can be defined by contrast:
   (a) Demand management sought to control inflation by keeping
demand at a non-inflationary level. If demand was excessive, the rule
was to deflate demand by fiscal or monetary policy, and thus to slow
the rise in output. The resulting lower pressure of demand would cause
inflation to be slower. In the process the rate of monetary growth was
likely also to be slowed down; but that was not the main aim (the main
aim was to control inflation), and was not considered an essential means
to that aim.
   (b) Monetary targetry sought to control the rate of monetary growth,
and that was thought to be the sufficient and necessary means to control
inflation. Monetary growth was to be controlled by manipulating
interest rates. A rise in interest rates was thought to work on the
demand-for-money schedule, and cause the public to wish to hold less
money (and, by inference, larger holdings of other financial assets). The
                                          Preface                                         vii
rise in interest rates would additionally cause slower output growth,
and that might help to slow monetary growth in the same way as at (a).
But the slowing of output was not in itself an aim of policy—the effect
on output was thought to be small and temporary—and the main way
to control money was directly via interest rates.
   These two kinds of policy should be sharply distinguished. Demand
management is not a specifically monetary policy at all: it could involve
the use of fiscal policy only. If it has effects on monetary growth, these
are side-effects and are not conceived as part and parcel of controlling
inflation; nor is the rate of monetary growth the criterion by which
policy is judged.
   In these respects, demand management is the opposite of monetary
targetry. Monetary targetry aims to control monetary growth directly,
and affects output growth only incidentally and only in a minor way.
Demand management, on the other hand, affects monetary growth only
as a side-effect of seeking to regulate real growth, and in this case it is
the former effect which is probably minor.
   Not only are the two sorts of policy distinct in concept; they were
equally clearly separate in practice. Manipulation of interest rates (as
John Fforde, a chief architect of the new policy in 1971, has explained)
was thought to offer a means of controlling the rate of monetary growth
without having much effect on output.2 A rise in interest rates was
thought to bring a fall in money holding along a demand-for-money
function, income being held constant. Nor would manipulation of output
growth have been thought a politically feasible way of controlling
monetary growth.3
   British monetary targetry, then, was not intended to operate via
manipulation of output growth. It follows that (as will be argued more
fully below) it could only operate by diverting financial flows by varying
the degree to which the flows passed through (or alternatively bypassed)
intermediation by the banks.
   This, then, is the policy approach with which we are concerned in
this book. Our concentration on this kind of policy dictates the whole
argument and underlies all our analysis, from the first to the last page
of the book. We ask how far this sort of diversion is possible, by what
means it is possible, and even whether it is possible at all. But the fact
that it is this sort of 'diversion' that we are concerned with has, I think,
not been understood by some of our critics.4

  3
     A loss of output growth on a politically prohibitive scale would have been needed to
bring a reduction of monetary growth on a scale which would still have been quite trivial:
see p. 138.
  4
     Though it does not appear from Laidler's review of our book, other articles make it clear
that he sees control of demand not only as a means to control of inflation but as a way of
controlling monetary growth. I agree about the former, but do not think the latter a feasible
viii                                       Preface
   The process of financial growth. The book starts with a picture,
presented in Chapter 2, of a growing economy in which the stock of
financial assets increases continually. Monetary assets are one part of
the total, and, if not prevented by policy action, the monetary stock
tends to grow with the total stock.
   In a growing economy the stock of financial assets is likely to grow
because saving and investment will be taking place and probably rising
in real terms; because saving will not all be done by the people who
invest; and because much of the growing debt between them will be
saleable financial assets. If the price of current goods and services is
rising rather than stable, the value of the stock of financial assets is
likely to rise faster. Thus rising nominal GDP is accompanied by a
rising value of the stock of financial assets.
   Part of the increase in the stock will consist of money (for practical
purposes, bank deposits). For lenders will find it convenient to hold a
fraction of the increments of their financial wealth in that form; and
borrowers will find it convenient to borrow in part via banks rather
than direct from lenders (for example, via the stock exchange). It is not
clear in the abstract whether the stock of money, or of financial assets
in total, is likely to grow faster or slower than, or as fast as, nominal
GDP. That is not crucial to the analysis. But this picture of roughly
parallel growth is, as it were, the 'primal scene' upon which we see the
authorities operating.5
   Our picture of policy intervention assumes that output growth and
inflation proceed as rapidly as before; consequently, that the growth of
the total stock of financial assets continues as before; consequently,
that restraint of monetary growth requires that the banking system
must be induced to make a smaller share of that growth consist of
money. Some part of the growth in the stock of financial assets that
would otherwise have taken the form of larger holdings of monetary
assets must be diverted instead to take the form of larger holdings of
non-monetary assets; and some of the investment that would otherwise
have been financed by means involving greater bank intermediation
must be funded instead by non-bank sources of finance.
or sensible way of proceeding. Cobham, in his review has also, I think, not understood the
question the book was addressing.
   5
     In Chap. 2 we argue that banks use rules of thumb relating to prospective borrowers'
incomes in deciding how much to lend, and for this reason argue that the overall stock of
bank loans will rise in line with aggregate nominal incomes. This is valid for the short term.
In the longer term it would be better to base the probability of roughly proportionate growth
on lenders' and borrowers' evolving preferences in a situation where incomes are rising—a
line of reasoning also found in Chap. 2, and implied above. Cobham in his review has us
argue that it is this behaviour of the banks that 'makes monetary growth itself essentially
endogenous'. That is not so. We argue that the authorities do not have the means to alter
the result that untrammelled market forces produce. Monetary growth is endogenous because
the authorities do not have the means to exogenize it.
                                          Preface                                         ix
   The question that Iain Saville and I examine in the book is essentially
whether monetary policy is capable of bringing about this sort of
diversion out of money which, in a relative sense, and in comparison
with what would otherwise have been, can be seen as a process of
disintermediation.
   What we did not examine is whether restrictive action by the monetary
authorities will not, also, slow down inflation, and hence slow down
also the growth in total financial assets which we take as a background
to our analysis. (We discussed methods of monetary control only in
later chapters of the book, without re-examining the initial description
of the financial process which we gave in Chapter 2.)
   Let us suppose that the authorities seek to control monetary growth
by varying interest rates. Leave aside the question whether this may be
effective, for that depends on the argument we are about to present.
Higher rates of interest would reduce demand and this, as we admit,
would slow inflation. This may seem to give our case away; but that is
not so.
   For the process, in our picture, would be a fairly lengthy one. It
would only be after some delay that higher rates of interest would slow
output growth, and they would only gradually produce a lower pressure
of demand, resulting only a little later still in a certain degree of
deceleration in inflation. This drawn-out process may be contrasted
with a picture in which prices are highly flexible, where higher rates of
interest very rapidly eliminate inflation (or radically reduce it), thus
also rapidly reducing the rate of growth of the stock both of total
financial assets and of money.
   The picture of slowly-working effects is surely the more realistic.
Interest rates have varied a good deal, and this kind of immediate and
decisive effect on inflation has not been evident. In the short term, then,
our general picture of the financial process seems valid.6 Empirical
evidence about the inflationary process will be discussed further at the
end of this note.

2. THE CONTROL OF MONETARY GROWTH

A main, practical question in dispute is whether monetary base control
(MBC) would work. Critics of the Bank of England argued at the time
of the British debates of 1980 (Laidler was among them) that the Bank
  6
     Our summary of our position was that 'in relating the course of the financial aggregates
to that of nominal GDP'—which we do in Chap. 2, dealing there with the short term—'we
take the course of output and prices as largely given, influenced only marginally by the pace
of bank lending, and confine ourselves to an analysis of how banks behave in this given
environment . . .' (p. 6). Cobham in his review seems to disagree with this assumption about
output and prices.
x                                            Preface
was mistaken—indeed, almost culpably mistaken7—in preferring to
continue attempting to control money by varying interest rates rather
than adopting base control. We argue that neither method can work—
for much the same reasons in each case.
   Our general conclusion, as Laidler rightly indicates, springs from our
view of interest-rate determination. We argue (pp. 138-9) that because
market expectations about future interest rates are diffuse and weakly
held, the central bank, though only a small market participant, is able
to exert great influence over expected rates of interest—and thus also
over current rates. But when it does so, all interest rates are affected;
the central bank has no systematic means of influencing relative interest
rates on different sorts of financial assets. No incentive is therefore
given to holders of broad money (which is interest-bearing) to move
into or out of near money, nor to banks to change the cost of bank
borrowing more than the cost of alternative sources of finance has
changed. Action by the central bank on interest rates thus causes no
(dis)intermediation into (out of) broad money, which continues to
evolve in response to market forces, just as it would if the central bank
had not intervened.
   Thus far David Laidler agrees with us. But he takes the view that 'a
narrow essentially non-interest bearing aggregate might be so controlled'.
Our argument to the contrary (p. 139) is that a single rise in interest
rates can induce only a single, once-for-all, portfolio shift out of
non-interest-bearing deposits. To control the rate of growth of a
monetary aggregate, however, the authorities must be able, if need be,
to counteract a continuing tendency for the aggregate to grow too fast.
This would require that they should be able to produce a continuing
series of portfolio shifts: and this in turn would require a series of rises
in interest rates. We argue that, even apart from political considerations,
there is a limit to how far the authorities can manipulate market
expectations and thus raise interest rates.
   The conclusion is that, even for narrow money, control of the rate
of monetary growth by means of variation in interest rates is possible
   7
      Laidler's comments on the British debate in 1980 seem to me unfair to the officials (not
myself) who were involved. The 'disagreement between politicians and the central bank' over
MBC, he says, 'was surely an honest one'. But, he adds, 'it is not surprising that there were
complaints at the time of Thatcher's policies being sabotaged by the bureaucracy'. MBC, he
says, 'was rejected by the Bank as unworkable on the basis of what it regarded as good
economic arguments, deeply rooted in the above-mentioned Keynesian tradition'. (As is clear
from the context, the latter means a belief that inflation was 'deeply entrenched', largely due
to cost pressures and largely independent of monetary policy.) This is unfair, since the decision
not to adopt MBC was made by the Prime Minister and the Chancellor of the Exchequer:
officials in the Treasury and the Bank gave only advice. The attitude of officials is less relevant
but was, I think, more pragmatic than theoretical. In 1980 the traditional control by
discretionary variation of interest rates still seemed a viable option, and the alternative seemed
untried and probably disruptive; and loss of control in the transition mattered.
                                          Preface                                         xi
 only as a short-term expedient. That may well be a relief to a beleaguered
 central bank governor, but it does not provide a permanent method of
 control. Nor is it appropriate to make it, as it is made in a great deal
 of macro-economic theory, into the anchor of the long-run equilibrium
 of the economic system.
   Monetary-base control. Our objection to MBC is not—as Laidler has
 mistakenly believed us to argue—'that it would force the Bank of
 England to give up its practice of making reserves available to the
 banks essentially on demand'. Our objection is rather that, even were
the Bank of England to give up this practice, base control would not
work. Laidler takes issue with our argument, however, on a point of
 some significance, namely on the assumptions about inflation which are
implicit in it.
   Take, first, MBC as applied to broad money. Advocates of MBC
argue that, if the authorities keep the banks short of reserves, the banks
will restrict the scale of bank intermediation.8 Our counter-argument is
as follows:
   • If banking were a single monopoly, the monopoly could, and
      might, simply restrict its lending. The result however would be
      disintermediation of the sort produced by direct controls, which is
      not what advocates of MBC want.
   • With numerous competing banks, each bank when short of reserves
      would seek to augment them by paying over the odds for borrowing
      on the wholesale money markets (pp. 34-7), thus driving up
      short-term interest rates. That would drive up all interest rates
      (especially if it is thought to happen by the wishes of the central
      bank).
   • The banks would presumably raise their deposit and lending rates.
      But since all interest rates would have risen, depositing with, or
      borrowing from, the banks would not be any less attractive than
      before relative to other sources of finance.
   • The stock of money, then, would not be reduced. The banks would
      therefore remain short of reserves in relation to deposits. Interest
      rates would be driven higher and higher, without offering the banks
      any escape from their dilemma.
   The argument thus is essentially the same as before. Because of
the way interest rates and interest-rate expectations are determined,
variations in interest rates, whether or not they are induced by BMC,
would not restrict the scale of bank intermediation and the quantity of
broad money. The argument for believing it would not work with
narrow money (pp. 149-51) is also the same as before—briefly, that it
  8
    The idea that the authorities can permanently keep the banks short of reserves is itself
not as simple and obvious as may appear: see pp. 148-9.
xii                               Preface
would only work for a while, till interest rates could be raised no
further.
   The monetarist answer to these arguments is to say that we have
made ourselves the prisoners of our view about inflation: we assume at
the outset that inflation is deeply entrenched and will continue little
changed whatever kind of monetary policy is adopted. But if the pace
of inflation were radically altered by the adoption of MBC, our
arguments would fall away. For if inflation did slow, MBC, even on
our view, would be found to be possible. This we seemed to admit
(p. 145). Our phrase was: 'If the adoption of base control itself quickly
moderated the pace of inflation, then the pressure behind the control,
and the banks' shortage of reserves, would be quickly eased, and interest
rates would not be driven high.' Laidler fastens on this admission:
indeed it is perhaps the central point in his review.
   In this passage we were, in fact, discussing not whether MBC could
conceivably work but whether interest rates would be driven higher in
the attempt. We concluded that, if inflation adjusted instantaneously,
they would not. In that case it would also follow (on our own argument)
that monetary growth also would as rapidly slow down. But that would
be because inflation was extremely sensitive to the influence of demand.
We could also have admitted that if inflation did not adjust in-
stantaneously, so that interest rates were driven up, then that would
deflate the economy and inflation would gradually decelerate.
   In either case, however, that would amount to demand management.
The slowing of inflation would come, not by controlling money, but by
deflating the economy; and it could be done by discretionary interest-rate
policy just as well as by base control (or, indeed, by fiscal policy). If,
in an effort to get quick effects, base control were imposed in a rough
and determined way, the likely result would be to create chaos in
financial markets.
   The issues here do not turn on empirical evidence about the flexibility
of prices. If prices were very flexible, inflation would be easy to control—
without resorting to base control. And if base control were nevertheless
used, the effect on inflation would come not through controlling
monetary growth, but by raising interest rates and thus controlling
demand.
3. MONETARY GROWTH AND INFLATION

There is also the question of whether, even if the authorities could
achieve the (intermediate) aim of controlling money, this would be
effective in the (ultimate) objective of controlling inflation. The question
is raised but not pressed in the book (p. 38), since, if the authorities
cannot control money, it is not a practical question. But the theoretical
question remains. Disintermediation into near substitutes for money is
                                    Preface                                 xiii
widely recognized as a trivial exercise, bringing a reduction in the money
stock as statistically measured, but accomplishing nothing of substance.
But disintermediation into less close substitutes (for example, equity
finance rather than bill finance or bank finance) differs from it only in
degree. Could not inflation proceed even if finance were endlessly
diverted—if one is concerned with broad money, to the extent, perhaps,
of freezing the scale of bank intermediation? Or, if narrow money is
what is thought to matter, by holding narrow money constant (in the
range for which this is possible) by raising interest rates so as to divert
monetary growth into broad money?
   What Laidler says on this question does not seem to me to take one
much further. If (he says) what he entitles the 'monetarist premise' (i.e.
that money fixes prices) were correct, then our analysis (which is that
money, as such, does not influence prices) would collapse. One way to
advance would indeed be, as he says, to interrogate the facts again. But
empirical proof on questions like this is hard to get; and what
would also be helpful would be a clearer account of the short-term
micro-foundations of the 'monetarist premise'.
   Laidler hails what we say of 'buffer stock' money as 'a new element
in British Keynesian thought' and as a bridge between that view and
recent monetarist thinking. But I doubt if we are so close. To me, our
version makes micro-sense, and the monetarist version does not.
   With respect to those who hold the opposite view, I would like to
be permitted to say that there seems to me a conjuring trick involved
in any version of the monetarist thesis, 'helicopter money' being only
the absurd, extreme example. The essential steps in the imagined process
are:
   (a) in an economy previously in balance, more money is 'created' by
the authorities;
   (b) the private sector's reaction to more money—by definition more
than the sector was previously content with (and would now still be
content with)—is to spend more;
   (c) greater spending raises prices.
The sleight of hand lies in the word 'created'. How much money an
agent in the private sector holds is neither a decision of the authorities,
nor the result of an action of a bank. It is the agent's own decision—
as is how many assets he holds of all sorts (the saving decision). How
is it, then, that he is first induced to decide to hold more money
than he wants; and then, having been so hoodwinked, he becomes
un-hoodwinked, and decides that what he now holds is too much?
   Laidler, it seems to me, instals the conjuring trick inside his theory
of 'buffer-stock' money:
Of course, even given a monetarist view of inflation, credit rationing can still
xiv                                        Preface
occur and changes in the lending habits of banks, such as [Dow and Saville]
discuss, are still possible; and of course such changes could influence demand
directly through credit market effects in much the way D and S suggest.
However, the deposits created as a by-product of bank lending, and initially
held in temporary 'buffer stocks', would exert their own independent influence
on demand when non-bank agents eventually tried to get rid of them, and
would continue to do so, not until the banks had been forced to adjust the
nominal size of their balance sheets, as would happen if the price level was
given, but until the price level had moved enough to reconcile the non-bank
public's portfolio choices with those of the banks.
   This seems to me paradoxical logic. The questions remain: how is it
that non-banks, having at one point acceded to holding more money,
later change their mind? And what happens if they do—a simple reversal
of the initial process of money creation, or something quite different—
a rise in the price level? It may be convenient to anticipate our answer
(pp. 27-30) to these two questions:
   (a) An increase in bank lending has resulted, let us say, in an increase
in spending and hence in real incomes. The latter increase is not at first
spent (which would be the second stage of the multiplier) but is held
for a while as money balances, most of which the holders will later
spend. Non-banks only seem to 'change their minds' about how much
money to hold because they are slow to react to the increase in their
income.
   (b) When the time comes for non-banks to spend their extra income
(held as abnormal money balances), this will force banks to reverse
their initial additional lending and initial creation of money. (Either
non-banks will decide, at the existing structure of relative asset prices,
to repay bank loans; or their relative distaste for money will so alter
relative asset prices as to induce debtors to banks to borrow from
non-bank sources and repay bank loans.)
   The ultimate grounds for our belief that the second adjustment takes
place are that the decision to save rather than spend is more fundamental
than the decision as to how to distribute holdings of wealth between
different classes of assets, and is not determined by the pattern of that
distribution.9
   9
     The above arguments should by themselves produce a constant ratio of money to income
in something more than the short term, as should Laidler's arguments. In the UK during the
period which we examined the ratio changed. We claim that there was a change in the banks'
lending criteria. But that alone is not enough: why was the additional lending not 'reversed'
by non-banks? For that, another sort of explanation is required. The most important of the
explanations we suggest are: (1) that it became less costly to borrow from banks with the
purpose of holding liquid balances; (2) that much borrowing from banks was by persons who
did not have access to non-bank forms of borrowing and thus were not induced to switch
out of money when relative asset prices changed. These factors would explain the 'breakdown'
of the demand for (broad) money function in the UK. I find them a more persuasive
explanation than those now suggested by C. A. E. Goodhart (1989), The Conduct of Monetary
Policy', Economic Journal, June.
                                         Preface                                       xv
4. PROPOSITIONS ABOUT INFLATION

David Laidler remarks that Iain Saville and I have shown that 'important
policy issues must hinge upon the position one takes about the causes
of inflation'—a question as he says once much debated and still not
settled. 'Would it be too much to hope', he asks, 'that they, or some
of the many British Keynesians who share their views, will now . . .
formulate precise testable hypotheses about the inflationary mechanism
. . . and present at least some preliminary evidence of their empirical
superiority to monetary ideas in the light of data generated in the last
fifteen years?' Then 'we could get down once again to the serious
business of advancing the empirical knowledge upon which further
progress . . . depends'.
   Evidence about the flexibility of prices. I will attempt only to isolate
the points on which empirical evidence would crucially affect the
argument, and discuss the sort of conclusions that might be drawn.
   Our picture of the process of financial growth—the natural scene on
which policy operates—requires that the scene itself should not be
radically altered when the authorities impinge on it. In particular, it
requires that even if inflation is slowed down when interest rates rise,
it should not be slowed down radically within a short period like a
year. As has already been argued, our argument about the impossibility
of monetary-base control, despite Laidler's focus on it, appears not to
be closely dependent on evidence about how quickly inflation responds
to policy.
   The evidence seems to be that prices in industrial countries are not
flexible in this sense. There seems reason to believe that cost pressures
go far to determine price changes in the short term, so that there is a
strong element of iteration and inertia in the inflation process, and only
limited room for sensitivity to demand influences. The 1975 survey
article to which Laidler refers in his review (of which he was part
author) reported general evidence of entrenched cost inflation,10 as did
Brown's 1985 (and his previous 1955) study.11 Evidence that monetary
policy can have a significant but more gradual effect on inflation is not
enough to overthrow this picture.
   Because prices have been inflexible in 'normal' periods, I do not
argue that they must always be so. Quite possibly half a century ago
prices were more flexible. In conditions of hyperinflation, also, prices
must become very flexible, because the opportunity cost of delayed cost
and price adjustments gets so high. 'All prices', as we say in the last
pages of the book (p. 240), 'would then tend to become progressively
  10
      D. Laidler and J. M. Parkin (1975), 'Inflation: A Survey', Economic Journal.
  11
      A. J. Brown, The Great Inflation (Oxford University Press, 1955) and World Inflation
since 1950 (Cambridge University Press, 1985).
xvi                                     Preface
more flexible, potentially more unstable, and more sensitive both to
demand pressures and to monetary policy
   The determinants of the price level. David Laidler asks for 'precise
testable hypotheses'. I find difficulty in formulating precise hypotheses
because I doubt whether inflation itself is a precisely regular process.
   I can understand why someone who is used to regarding money as
the anchor of the price level should turn to us and say: 'If not money,
what is?' But having abolished one anchor, we do not offer another.
We do not see the price level tending to some equilibrium point, any
more than most of us see total output doing so; nor are we much more
clear what governs the speed of inflation than economists usually are
about what governs the pace of growth. Economic theory predicts that
prices rise in response to excess demand, but says little about how fast.
The speed of inflation does not seem rooted in some fundamental
feature of human nature, on the basis of which one could construct an
adequate micro-theory.
   Before the 1950s, few attempted empirical explanation of the rate of
inflation, or thought it possible. Fashion since then has swung to the
other extreme of unbounded empirical confidence. Phillips's (1958)
results12 have been taken as revealing a natural law, and his approach
has been built on and progressively sophisticated. While Leslie Dicks-
Mireaux and I got comparable results, these (as we tried to say at the
time) seemed to us quite possibly built on sand, not well-grounded on
micro foundations and no more than a description, temporarily true,
of the behaviour of one society at one point in time.13 I have also
remained sceptical whether the evidence that demand pressures mattered
could be extended to validate the concept of an 'equilibrium' demand
level. It has been common to formulate the influence of demand on
inflation in terms of the rate of unemployment at which inflation does
not accelerate (NAIRU); but the critical level seems to have increased
progressively as unemployment grew.14 That perhaps provides some
guide to policy (namely, if you want to moderate inflation, increase
unemployment for a while). But it is theoretically unsatisfactory since
it leaves an incomplete explanation of inflation: if there is, indeed, a
level of unemployment at which (at this particular date) the rate of
inflation will be stable, what causes that critical level to change?
Ingenuity can doubtless propose answers. But it is not clear that a
procedure of adding subtlety to subtlety is the way to construct a
  12
     A. W. Phillips (1958), 'The Relation between Unemployment and the Rate of Change
of Money Wage Rates in the United Kingdom 1861-1957, Economica.
  13
     See L. A. Dicks-Mireaux and J. C. R. Dow (1959), The Determinants of Wage Inflation,
United Kingdom, 1945-56', and L. A. Dicks-Mireaux (1961), 'The Interrelationship between
Cost and Price Changes, 1946-1959'.
  14
     See for instance Layard and Nickell (1985)
                                    Preface                                 xvii
convincing description of a world which does not appear very tidy in
the first place.
5. CONCLUDING REMARKS

The kind of monetary policy that is formulated in terms of monetary
targets has been called 'practical monetarism'. I have sought to
differentiate it sharply from demand management: it aims to control
inflation by controlling the rate of monetary growth, not by controlling
the pressure of demand—which is different, even if monetary control
has marginal or temporary effects on total demand.
   Laidler's own kind of monetarism—the intellectual position from
which he reviews our book—is not, I think, quite the same as the kind
of monetarism that the book is about (since his effects come from
controlling demand). Some readers may regard this kind of monetarism,
as we have defined it, as an extreme and unusual definition of monetary
policy, which can hardly justify the attention we give it. Indeed, having
(I think) now given a more precise definition of 'monetary targetry', it
seems to me to become obvious almost by definition that it is not an
approach which should ever have been expected to work, nor been
adopted as a feasible approach. Though one might think that its failure
has now been clearly demonstrated, it has nevertheless not yet been
very clearly and decisively rejected in public debate. There remain many
in all our countries, economists and non-economists alike, who think
that controlling monetary growth is the direct and obvious means to
control inflation, and that controlling monetary growth is the only
proper object of monetary policy. It is therefore worth examining
carefully the experience with monetary targets in the decade since the
mid-1970s, and analysing the reasons why, in Britain at least, it so
clearly failed.
   'The price level and the role of money', I remarked in my previous
book, 'happen to be foci of dispute in economists' rival pictures of the
world' and I added:
Argument between those who set out from different starting points is however
always difficult. What one would ideally like from one's opponent would be
suspension of his disbelief in one's own argument, so that he could view the
flux of events in terms of one's own categories, and see how well the facts then
fit. It is asking a lot to expect one's opponent to see the world, even
experimentally, through one's own eyes . . .' (MBE 268)
   David Laidler has gone far to (temporarily) seeing things through our
eyes, and by doing so has made it much easier to conduct a useful
discussion.
   Cobham's review, though disparaging, needs less comment because
he seems to fail to notice what our central argument is. Having argued
xviii                                       Preface
in detail, as we did, that the authorities lack adequate means to control
the money stock, and in particular that base control would be
ineffective—neither of which points has I think been seriously argued
previously—it is disappointing to be told that we 'add little to the
debate on alternative techniques of monetary control (e.g. monetary
base control) . . .'; or that we merely restate 'traditional Keynesian
views on money
   On the plane of general theory, Keynes's principal insight was in my
view to see unemployment as a result of market imperfections, not of
market perfections, and as arising not from malfunctioning of the
labour market but from the inability of interest rates to equilibrate
saving and investment at full employment. Our analysis is in this spirit
and as Laidler says has 'a strong claim to be regarded as reflecting an
authentic development of the central ideas of the General Theory'. But
unlike Keynes, and unlike most Keynesians15—indeed, perhaps, unlike
most economists—we see the money stock determined not as a policy
variable but, like other financial stocks, by market forces.
   Cobham rebukes us for 'inadequate familiarity with much of the
more recent literature'. In each of the three examples he gives, the
charge is, I think, misdirected;16 but it is true that our argument is not
closely based on modern academic teaching which, in some respects,
we were trying to go beyond. Our concern was not only to criticise a
certain line of policy, but, more positively, to explain aspects of the
real world not previously well-explained: in particular, how central
banks, small though they are, yet have considerable power over interest
rates and exchange rates. It is as corollary of the resulting analysis of
central bank power that we deduce its limitations.
/. C. R. Dow
National Institute of Economic and Social Research
October 1989.
   15
      One exception was Kaldor, who however only asserted that money was endogenous but
gave no reasons. Another was Hicks in his later phase.
   16
      Cobham gives three examples: 1. We should, he says, have mentioned the argument that
exchange-rate overshooting is less likely if money is held as a buffer, since interest rates then
adjust less rapidly. But we take issue with both the normal account of how the money stock
is determined and the normal account of buffer-stock money, on which this argument rests.
2. In our discussion of fiscal policy, we should, he says, have made 'more explicit reference'
to 'the debate arising out of Sargent and Wallace's work on the monetary-fiscal policy link
and the debt/income ratio'. But that, too, assumes that the price level can be determined by
controlling the monetary base, which is what we were at pains to deny; and Cobham's criticism
would, again, seem to reflect his not being aware how radical our argument is. 3. In our
discussion of overfunding we should, he says, have referred to a paper by Andrew Bain
prepared for the Bank of England's Panel of Academic Consultants in 1982. It was not
referred to, not because I was unaware of it, but because I happen not to have thought the
paper helpful: as Economics Director at the Bank I was responsible for and attended the
Panel meetings.
                               Preface

I was Economics Director at the Bank of England from early 1973 until
early 1981, and for the next three years performed the same role as
Economic Adviser to the Governor. Thus, I saw in, and slightly
outstayed, Lord Richardson's ten years as Governor of the Bank, and
saw events from within the Bank for most of the period covered by this
study.
   The book is a co-operative effort by myself and Iain Saville. He was
formerly a member of the Bank's Economic Section, and was granted
leave of absence by the Bank in accordance with its policy of encouraging
its staff to move for periods to work outside. However, he returned to
the Bank when the work was half done; its completion and revision thus
had to be carried out without his full assistance, and the drafting is
mostly mine. Nevertheless, this study of monetary policy is very much a
joint result of our collaboration, and represents our shared conclusions.
The essence of the argument is contained in Chapters 2, 3, 4, and 11.
These especially are the product of close discussion between us and
repeated exchanges of drafts, to the point where, as with all successful
collaborations, it is impossible for either to feel sure what each
contributed. After Iain Saville left Robert Anderton acted as research
officer, and we are both indebted to him for his help with the task of
completing the work.
   Our analysis leads us to the conclusion that the ten-year experiment
with monetary targets was pursuit of a false trail. Grounds for some
disillusion were apparent as early as 1973 when the Corset was imposed.
Since then, the difficulty of controlling bank lending has become
increasingly obvious, and our work may be seen essentially as an analysis
of the implications of that observation. That, however, is not the same as
saying that we felt or knew at the time what we feel or think we know
now.
   The advice we offered, and the views we held five or ten years ago,
cannot, then, be inferred from what we say now. That may not be
obvious and may easily be ignored, and I will cite one illustration.
Although the book implies various objections to the idea of monetary
targets, when targets were announced in 1976 I was among those most in
favour of the innovation, which many then doubted. My reason was,
admittedly, not that it would be counter-inflationary; rather that, in face
of an evident tendency by the Bank simply to accommodate to events, it
would lead the monetary authorities to define their aim and thus have a
policy.
xx   Preface

   One would like to think one changes one's views because one learns
more. In the first postwar decade, monetary policy was usually thought
to have a minor role, and in my previous book I could see only a small
place for it in the management of the economy. Contrary to this
impression, when I went to work at OECD (which I did for the ten years
from 1963), it seemed that in other countries monetary policy was
nevertheless influential, and I was persuaded that it must be more
important than I had thought. The present book suggests that its true
role is, as of old, the management not of demand, but of the exchange
rate.
   In their confrontation before the Macmillan Committee, Montague
Norman is usually depicted as hopelessly out-manoeuvred and wrong,
barely able to grasp what Keynes was saying. I now wonder whether it
was not Montague Norman who was right, inarticulate though he was on
that occasion; and whether the large impact on activity that Keynes
attributed to changes in the short-term rate of interest (on which so much
has since been built), though clever and ingenious, was not simply
mistaken, and Norman right to be puzzled. The use of interest rates for
domestic purposes rather than the exchange rate may come to seem one
of the greatest aberrations of the half-century since.
   The present book is not what was first intended. The aim was
originally a broader study of the issues raised by current economic
policy, in which monetary policy would have only a section. Of that
wider study, only part has been completed; chiefly, the econometric
study by Iain Saville and Kevin Gardiner of the causes of recession and
inflation in the United Kingdom since the early 1970s, a summary of
which has been published in the National Institute Economic Review for
August 1986. It was soon clear, however, that monetary policy and
monetary theory would require a separate book. The subject was too
complex for anything less, discussion of it at all levels too diffuse and too
various, the role that monetary policy had come to be allotted in
economic strategy too dominant, and the handling of what would have to
be an involved argument too demanding on authors and readers alike.
   The book is based throughout on published sources, and in view of our
different connections with the Bank, we wish to state that the Bank of
England has no responsibility for it. Somewhat as a court jester is
licensed to voice unpopular truths, an economic adviser is paid to give
honest advice. To examine critically the policies followed by the Bank, as
we here do, is thus not to criticize the Bank; and is better seen as an act of
possible utility than of disloyalty to an institution in whose work we have
taken part.
   The world has moved on as we were writing, and in this country
monetary targets have been largely abandoned. Some proclaim that this
 kind of monetary policy is dead; for others, perhaps, there has been an
                                                               Preface   xxi

uneasy forced retreat. Our view, as it has developed, is that it ought never
to have been believed either possible or useful in the first place. Since we
have found it difficult to set out the argument fully and clearly, and since
after a decade of living with monetary targetry it now seems to be time to
take stock and consider what other line of policy to pursue, we hope that
the book may contribute to a discussion in this and other countries which
will no doubt continue. Our thesis in some respects runs counter to
prevailing economic theory, and is perforce addressed largely to
economists. The argument is however of general importance, and we
have tried to make it, at least in outline, intelligible more generally. We
hope, then, that the book will interest various sorts of reader, including
those now responsible for the conduct of monetary policy, and — among
those who formerly were — the Governor, whom those of us who
worked with him knew we were lucky to serve, and who, with his own
great mastery and style, presided over the monetary events of that
decade.
   We have to thank Michael Artis, John Fleming, Andrew Britton, and
an anonymous critic who read and commented on the whole of the draft,
and Brian Henry, Bryan Hopkin, and Christopher Allsopp who read
parts. I have also to thank my son James for help with two passages. A
book such as this, dealing with a broad theme and touching on many
fields where a vast amount has been written, inevitably exposes the
authors to risks of faults of both omission and commission. We are
conscious that huge deficiencies must remain, but without these critics
the book would certainly have been worse.
   The study has made been possible by grants from the Nuffield
Foundation, the Leverhulme Trust, and the Gatsby Charitable
Foundation. We acknowledge our debt to these bodies, and also to Sir
David Scholey of S. G. Warburg & Co. for his friendly offices in getting
the project started. The National Institute of Economic and Social
Research agreed to administer the grants and provide a home for the
project, and for this purpose kindly made me a Visiting Fellow. No base
could have been more congenial, and we owe much to Andrew Britton
and Kit Jones, the Director and Secretary of the Institute, and all who
assisted our work. In particular, we thank Julia Salisbury, our secretary
and assistant, for lending her talents with such willing grace.

                                     1
                             Introduction

This book is an essay in theory, and a study of monetary policy in
practice. It is an examination of how monetary policy has worked, or
failed to work, in the United Kingdom in the 1970s and 1980s. This
involves questions of monetary theory; the book is theoretical more than
historical, and bears on the general question of the effects to be
attributed to monetary policy at any time.

The point we start from
This study has its origins in observation of how the operation of
monetary policy looks to those working in a central bank, and is an
attempt to make sense of that experience. The central bank is only one
operator among many in financial markets, and those responsible for
conducting monetary policy are invariably fully aware of the strength of
market forces and their limited power to influence the outcome. The
actual financial operations conducted by a central bank are trivial in scale
compared with the monetary and financial aggregates it seeks to
influence. On the face of it, it seems unlikely that small-scale actions
should be capable of profound and decisive consequences for national
output and the general price level.
   These perceptions contrast with the powerful effect attributed to
monetary policy in political discussion and in monetary theory. Despite
the limited scale of their operations, the power of the authorities to affect
the behaviour of financial markets — at least within a range — is
nevertheless clearly considerable. Among the questions to be explained is
how that power arises, and how deep it goes, i.e. how far it extends
beyond financial markets, and thus has important repercussions on the
working of the economy as a whole.
   These questions were raised by observation of events in the United
Kingdom, more especially since 1971. The removal in that year of ceilings
on bank lending, which in stricter or looser form had existed since the
war, marked at least in intention a clean break from one type of
monetary policy to another. For most of the period since then, the
authorities have been concerned to control the rate of growth of the
monetary aggregates, a concern expressed from 1976 onwards in the
yearly announcement of targets for the rate of monetary growth in the
year ahead. The study thus is concerned most directly with the type of
2   Introduction

monetary policy that seeks to control the rate of monetary growth. The
possibilities of policies with a different orientation are discussed at the
end of the book.
  Our initial aim was to take a close look at the different methods of
monetary control used by the authorities, and to examine the effects they
could be expected to have on monetary conditions and on the economy
generally. It was quickly obvious, however, that that required a prior
account of what it was that the authorities were seeking to control,
namely, an account of the nature of monetary growth. Since most of
what we call broad money consists of the liabilities of the banks, we
needed a fuller account than appeared to be available of the dynamic
aspects of the behaviour of the banking system as a whole. We have
therefore sought to explain the forces that, within the setting of the rest
of the financial system, cause the collective balance sheet of the banking
system to expand in an economy where nominal incomes tend to grow
progressively.
   Our broad strategy thus has been, first, to try to provide an account of
the behaviour of the financial system in the absence of official
intervention or control, and then, as a second stage, to superimpose the
effects of official acts of control. That is what Parts I and II in turn
attempt.

Some comments on our general approach
Since the argument of successive chapters is interconnected, it will
probably be helpful at the outset to give a foretaste of the argument and
make some general comments about it.
   Our analysis treats lending by financial institutions as the driving force
of monetary growth. It is convenient for much of the time to concentrate
on lending by banks. Since this is broadly matched by bank deposits,
which are the main component of sterling M3 (£M3; after 1987 called
simply M3), we give emphasis to that aggregate, which we refer to as
'broad money' — and which, perhaps for somewhat different reasons,
has been the focus of policy in the United Kingdom. Deposits with
building societies are, however, close substitutes for time deposits with
banks, and are likely to become closer as building societies provide fuller
banking services. Much of what we have to say about bank lending
therefore applies also to lending by building societies; and we also give
emphasis to a wider aggregate including deposits with building societies,
which we refer to as 'very broad money'. (This we take to be roughly
represented by PSL2 — after 1987 called M5 — which includes cash and
all bank deposits together with relatively liquid building society deposits
and the small amount of money-market instruments that is held by the
non-bank private sector.)
                                                           Introduction   3

   In most theoretical discussion, and in most macroeconomic models
that incorporate money, the supply of 'money' is treated as exogenous,
i.e. as determined not by the working of the economic system but from
outside, by decision of the monetary authorities. The assumption is that
the central bank can readily control the volume of bank deposits. The
banks, it is assumed, maintain reserves in the form of balances at the
central bank; the reserves they need to maintain are assumed to be related
to the size of their deposits; and the central bank can determine the size
of their reserves. Hence the central bank decides the size of their
deposits, and if it supplies the banks with an increase in reserves, bank
deposits will expand by a multiple of that increase.
   In our analysis of monetary growth, our strategy is to look first at the
factors determining the growth of bank lending on the assumption that
the authorities make no attempt to control the process. At this stage
therefore we also assume that the authorities follow an accommodating
policy with regard to banks' reserves, and allow the banks themselves to
decide, in the light of the costs and advantages as seen by them, what
balances to hold at the central bank. Only later do we examine the
consequences of monetary-base control. To those accustomed to the
standard analysis, this procedure will appear perverse in omitting what to
them will appear an essential element.
   The reasons justifying our procedure are these. In the first place, this
treatment is realistic; for in fact, the authorities have always stood ready
to provide liquidity in the form of balances at the Bank of England, in
essentially unlimited quantities though at a price of the authorities'
choosing. They have not allocated a predetermined quantity of reserves
at whatever price was required to clear the market, but rather have varied
the price at which banks were permitted to replenish an insufficiency of
reserves. In the second place, it is questionable whether base control
could provide effective monetary control. Since the effects of such
control are difficult to analyse, the argument is best presented, as we
have laid it out, in two stages. We hope that, to those willing to wait till
the end of the argument, the initial assumption of an accommodating
policy will appear a legitimate device.
   The terminology used in discussing money creation itself may carry
theoretical implications. For those who see the money stock as being
easily controlled by the control of bank reserves, the demand for money
derives from private preferences, but the supply of money is an official
decision. The stock of money allowed by the authorities is regarded as
the money 'supply', and is often called by that term; and an excess of
'supply' over demand can be seen as in 'injection' of money into the
economic system, from which further important consequences are
traced. In our exposition we avoid the term 'the money supply' because
of its connotation of exogeneity, and speak simply of the money stock;
4   Introduction

we use the term in inverted commas only in referring to other analysis
where that connotation is intended.
   Our account of how the money stock is determined is coloured by the
developments that in fact occurred, and is meant to explain them. In
fact, it early became clear that the main difficulty in controlling
monetary growth lay in controlling bank lending. The course of sterling
bank lending and of broad money is shown schematically in Fig. 1.1,
which shows bank lending and broad money as ratios of GDP. Expressed
in these terms, there was a surge in bank lending after the removal of
lending control in 1971 that continued through 1972 and 1973. In the
years 1974-80, when the Corset control was intermittently in force, there
was first a contraction and then a more level trend. After 1980 there was
again a fairly rapid rise.




FIG. 1.1 Bank lending and broad money, selected years, 1970-1984
                                                           Introduction   5

   We clearly have to give weight to the influence of direct controls before
 1971 and of the Corset; but how much influence one ascribes to them
depends in part on how one sees the system working without them. Our
initial approach was to regard other types of monetary policy not as
determining the pace of monetary growth, but as one influence among
others which modified an endogenous process of money creation by the
banks. It is mostly by variations of central-bank rate that the authorities
sought to influence monetary growth; but closer analysis suggested that it
neither was nor could be expected to be effective for that purpose. Thus,
despite our initial presumption, we conclude that, apart from the
possible influence of direct lending controls, the historical growth of the
aggregates was largely endogenous and was determined primarily by
bank lending.
   We see banks rationing the size of loans to individual customers not
out of inertia, but as an equilibrium response; and we argue that the rules
of thumb adopted by the banks in determining loans are related to their
customers' prospective incomes. Hence (provided lending criteria remain
unchanged), aggregate bank lending to the private sector will grow with
nominal GDP. It is for this reason that we present historical
developments in the form of ratios of the aggregates to GDP, and, with
some reservations, interpret a change in the ratios as evidence of a change
in the banks' lending criteria. We see lending criteria as fairly stable and
subject only to gradual change; but it is difficult to make sense of what
occurred without supposing that large shifts occurred. We see banks as
able to vary their lending, however, only within limits set by the
availability of substitute channels of lending. Thus though banks have
some freedom to vary lending and hence the stock of money, this is true
only within a certain range.
   In much macroeconomic theory, the twin assumptions of exogenous
money 'supply' and stable money demand mean that money 'supply'
controls the price level — either continuously except for temporary
divergencies (as in monetarist models); or in the equilibrium long term
(as in other macroeconomic models). If, as on our view, the money stock
is largely or wholly endogenous, it cannot provide a nominal 'anchor'
that controls the price level. To this basic question we revert in our two
final chapters (and there give reasons why we do not discuss many
propositions stemming from the postulate of rational expectations that
have figured in recent discussion).
   For this reason we see the development of output and prices as largely
dependent on factors other than the rate of monetary growth. As noted
above, we see banks within certain limits being able to vary bank lending
at choice, and we see the expenditure made possible by such variations in
bank lending as influencing output and prices, but not being a dominant
influence. For these reasons, in relating the course of the financial
6   Introduction

aggregates to that of nominal GDP, we take the course of output and
prices as largely given, influenced only marginally by the pace of bank
lending, and confine ourselves to an analysis of how banks behave in this
given environment; we have not regarded it as essential to our task to
provide an explanation of what determines that environment. Had we
been constructing a model of long-term equilibrium, we would probably
have taken bank lending in the long term to be completely endogenous,
dependent on the course of output and prices, and the latter as
determined entirely by non-monetary factors. This general approach
appears similar to that of Kaldor (1980).
   We have also had to be concerned with interest rates. For the
authorities have tried to control monetary growth largely by changes in
what used to be called Bank rate and which we call central-bank rate.
Most theoretical discussion of interest rates has been in equilibrium terms
and relates to the long term. To supplement this, our purposes forced us
to propose our own account of the various influences, including that of
central-bank rate, that determine the general level of interest rates in the
short term.
   In the long term, the (real) rate of interest is usually seen as determined
by the marginal productivity of the stock of capital and the desire by
consumers to hold wealth. In such a theory there appears no room for
interest rates to be directly influenced by central-bank rate. To provide
room for that we argue that, within a range, interest rates are
indeterminate in terms of the factors that figure in long-run equilibrium
theory; these however remain important, in that they set bounds to the
short-term fluctuation of interest rates. Within this range, we argue that
market opinion about future interest rates can be influenced by central-
bank rate, which is nevertheless an instrument of limited capability; if
pushed too far, it would cease to seem to market operators a credible
policy, and thus would lose its grip over market forces. Our account of
the determination of exchange rates, which we have also to discuss, is in
broadly analagous terms.
   To see the system as (within limits) indeterminate in this sense may
seem unduly to play down the market-type relationships on which
economists typically build. But it is not a dismissal of rationality, which
is the basic postulate of economic theory. The rational response to large
uncertainty about the future may result in a loose-jointed economic
system in which the ultimate equilibrium point is a range that does not
rigidly fix current prices and quantities.
   Various forms of uncertainty have an important place at most stages
of our account. Not only is money held as a hedge against uncertainty; its
creation, via the granting of new loans, depends on the attitudes of banks
towards the credit worthiness of borrowers, which is impossible to assess
objectively. Because of uncertainty, both capital markets and exchange
                                                            Introduction    1

markets are, we argue, in some broad sense imperfect. That produces a
range of indeterminacy in both interest rates and the exchange rate, and
consequently also gives scope for the authorities to influence them.
Within this range, the course of interest rates and exchange rates is
determined by the guesses of markets regarding the uncertain future
evolution of policy, of economic events, and of other participants' views.
   Our discussion is not couched in algebraic terms, primarily because it
did not appear necessary for the kind of arguments we wished to make.
This is partly because of the kind of reality we are describing. While
mathematical treatment is legitimate where 'risks' can be quantitatively
evaluated, it is certainly more difficult in the case of the more elusive
types of 'uncertainty' which seem in fact to prevail (Knight 1921). An
illustration of the limitations of mathematical treatment is the failure of
econometric work on the exchange rate to model the risk premium, noted
later in Chapter 5 (Section 4).
   To the extent that this study is novel, there is the question of how
firmly based it can claim to be. It would be difficult to find full empirical
verification for propositions of the sort we advance. There is, however,
wide disagreement among economists on all aspects of economic theory.
Econometric research, too, is no better based than the theories it tests.
Though our conclusions must clearly be tentative, they are based on close
observation and analysis and may appear worth critical examination.
   The potential scope of this study being large, it may be helpful to note
what we have not attempted. In describing the macroeconomic working
of the financial system and the influence of monetary policy on it, we
have discussed only those aspects of the microeconomic behaviour of
financial institutions that appeared essential for that purpose. We have
not sought to provide a picture of all aspects of the behaviour of a typical
individual bank, or to discuss in detail the behaviour of different
financial markets. For our purpose, therefore, only part of the extensive
literature (reviewed for instance in Cuthbertson 1985) appeared relevant.
The most important propositions we advance are not such as to be
capable of econometric testing, and we refer to econometric results much
more briefly than for instance Spencer (1986). On questions where there
has been recent debate, we have not attempted a full review of the
discussion, but confined ourselves to points (for instance on buffer-stock
money) where we had something to add, or where this would clarify the
implications of the view we were putting forward.
   A detailed history of monetary policy in this country since the
mid-1960s would have required a different sort of book, and we have
tried to give only a brief outline of the high points. Fuller accounts are to
be found, for the early period, in Blackaby et al. (1978); and for later
years for instance in Artis and Lewis (1981), in Gowland (1982), in the
collection of material from the Bank of England Quarterly Bulletin (in
8    Introduction

Bank of England 1984), and (though this is part only of their aim) in
Goodhart (1984) and Spencer (1986). A particular reason for not
attempting a fuller historical account here is that it is in fact difficult to
write of what the authorities did without either appearing to endorse the
intentions of policy and the theory on which it was based, or inserting
frequent disclaimers of a sort that tend to make the account seem
pointless. To unravel how policy really operated, deeper analysis is
needed than can be embodied in a chronological narrative.

The plan of the book
The bulk of the study is concerned with analysis of how the financial
system behaves, and the effect on it of intervention by the monetary
authorities. It is only in the last chapter that we are concerned with policy
prescription; elsewhere it is touched on only incidentally.
   The study has three main parts, discussing, respectively, how the
financial system behaves, how policy impinges on it, and finally, the
implications of the analysis for theory and for policy. The first three
chapters of Part I contain the essential features of our analysis. For
simplicity, that is first set out in terms of the reactions of the private
sector (abstracting from government transactions) and of a closed
economy (abstracting from transactions with other countries):
    • Chapter 2 discusses the behaviour of the banking system as a
      channel for the flow of funds between ultimate lenders and ultimate
      borrowers.
    • Chapters 3 and 4 discuss the behaviour of interest rates.
The following two chapters extend the analysis to include the effects of
government transactions and transactions with overseas. That modifies
the analysis only in minor ways; these chapters are thus to some extent
digressions from the main line of argument:
    • Chapter 5 considers the effect on interest rates of external
      influences, involving a general discussion of the determinants of
      floating exchange rates.
    • Chapter 6 considers the impact of public-sector financial
      operations, and in particular of public-sector borrowing from the
      banks.
We are then in a position to examine in Part II the impact of policy.
    • Chapters 7-10 analyse the impact of the various methods of
      monetary control that have been attempted since 1971 or, in the case
      of monetary base control, seriously considered.
    • Chapter 11 draws on the earlier analysis to offer a broad
                                                          Introduction   9

     explanation of monetary developments in this country since the
     1960s.
Part III then concludes as follows:
  • Chapter 12 examines the general theoretical implications of the
    analysis.
  • Chapter 13 considers the implications for monetary policy in future.
  The analysis in the study casts doubt both on the possibility of
controlling the rate of growth of broad monetary aggregates by anything
short of direct controls, and, more generally, on the rationale of a policy
couched in the terms of the aggregates. The most important conclusion
of Chapter 13 is that monetary policy should aim not to control the
aggregates but to secure a fairly stable exchange rate.
This page intentionally left blank
             PART I

The Behaviour of the Financial System
This page intentionally left blank
                                     2
    The Macroeconomic Behaviour of the Banking
                     System

This chapter analyses how, in an economy with rising money incomes,
the stock of money tends to grow. This analysis is required as a basis for
our later discussion of monetary control, which can be regarded as
modifying the working of the market forces that cause the money stock
to grow. The discussion will be mostly in terms of broad money (£M3 —
after 1987 called simply M3), or of very broad money, including deposits
with building societies (of which we take PSL2 — after 1987 called M5 —
as an indicator). Deposits with building societies are very like deposits
with banks, and building societies are here regarded as a specialized type
of bank. Although the discussion in principle embraces both sorts of
institution, for convenience we will often refer only to banks.
   Broad money (or very broad money) consists for the most part of
deposits with banks (or banks and building societies). The counterpart of
such deposits mainly takes the form of lending to the private sector.
What this chapter attempts therefore is a general account of the
behaviour of the banking system in response to the preferences of
borrowers and lenders in the private sector. Most of the remainder of
bank lending (nowadays much the smaller part) is to the public sector.
The scale of such lending is essentially under the authorities' control
(with qualifications noted later) and will be discussed not in this chapter
but in Chapter 6, along with other questions about the management of
public debt.
   The banking system provides a major channel of finance between final
lenders (who are predominantly in the personal sector) and final
borrowers. (Among private borrowers companies formerly predomi-
nated, but persons have now become equally important.) Banks'
operations involve them in risks of various sorts, including the risk of
withdrawals of deposits; to guard against these latter risks, banks need to
hold reserves of relatively liquid assets. Balances at the central bank are,
together with cash, the most liquid asset available. Treatment of the
money stock as within the control of the authorities may seem justified
by the fact that the authorities can influence the size of balances at the
central bank, or at least make them more costly to the banks. But (for
reasons explained in Chapter 1) the analysis in this chapter will assume
that the banks are left free to choose the level of their reserves, and that
penal interest rates are not imposed when the central bank provides
14   The Behaviour of the Financial System

reserves as necessary. (The operation and consequences of official action
to put pressure on the banks' reserves is discussed later, in Chapters 8
and 9.)
   In the following discussion (for reasons also noted in Chapter 1), we
take the growth of real output and the pace of inflation as given and try
to give an account of why, against this background, there is likely to be a
progressive expansion of banks' balance sheets, and hence of the stock of
money. There is a considerable literature on the behaviour of individual
banks in a static environment, but no generally agreed description of this
collective dynamic process; the argument in this chapter is thus
necessarily tentative. We have tried to give an account that includes the
broad considerations to which theory would point, and also gives due
emphasis to factors that seem to have been important.
   Section 1, after noting the scale of banks' activities in the context of
the whole financial system, goes on to discuss some features of the role of
banks that are important for their collective behaviour; Section 2
discusses what determines the scale of bank intermediation; and Section
3, to exemplify the working of the system, examines the effects of an
exogenous change in bank lending behaviour. Section 4 then discusses in
greater detail the relation between banks and the wholesale money
markets. As will be apparent, this chapter provides only a first statement,
which will leave much to be filled in in following chapters.

              1. THE PLACE AND FUNCTIONS OF BANKS

Most money consists of bank deposits. At mid-1985, the stock of broad
money held by the public (£M3) was nearly £120 billion, of which notes
and coin amounted to only about £12 billion (Table 2.1). Notes and coin
are in effect an interest-free loan to the government, and, if thus
considered a liability, are a liability of a body 'outside' the financial
system. Bank deposits, on the other hand, are the liability of institutions
'inside' it, namely the banks. What we have on this distinction been
taught to call 'inside money' (Gurley and Shaw 1960, 73) amounts to
nearly nine-tenths of the total. The relative importance of bank deposits
has been increasing (from somewhat over 80 per cent of broad money in
1970 to over 90 per cent in 1985). The stock of very broad money (PSL2
— which includes most deposits with building societies and some other
relatively liquid assets) was just over £200 billion at mid-1985, two-thirds
as much again as broad money.
   The total of financial assets of all sorts is many times larger. On the
classification adopted in Table 2.2, the total at the same date was nearly
£1,900 billion; if one nets out financial intermediaries, the total was
about £900 billion. Table 2.3 shows in greater detail the major items
among the financial assets and indebtedness of the main sectors.
